========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in. I, um... I hope I'm in the right place. It’s probably nothing too serious, but I just wanted to make sure I’m doing the right thing by coming in. 

Well, I work at a front desk—I'm a receptionist—and what really pushed me to make the appointment is that... it's a little embarrassing, but every time a door slams loud, or if someone rolls a suitcase really fast across the lobby, I just... I jump. Like, I really jump, almost like it’s happening again. And then it takes me a minute or so to just get my heart rate back down to normal. 

I try to hide it so people don't think I'm acting strange, but it's been bothering me. Is that... is that something you see people for? I just want to make sure I'm not overreacting by being here.

Well, um... I wouldn't say I avoid *everywhere*, exactly. I try really hard not to let it affect my work too much, you know? I want to be a good employee. But... I guess I have been changing my routine just a little bit. 

Like, instead of walking through the main lobby where... where it happened, I've been taking the back hallway to get to the breakroom or the restroom. It's just... walking past that specific spot, it makes my chest feel a little tight, and I just get this strong urge to get away from it. So the back hallway is just easier right now.

It’s probably nothing too serious, but I just wanted to make sure I’m doing the right thing by coming in. \n\nWell, I work at a front desk—I'm a receptionist—and what really pushed me to make the appointment is that... it's a little embarrassing, but every time a door slams loud, or if someone rolls a suitcase really fast across the lobby, I just... I jump. Like, I really jump, almost like it’s happening again. Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm completely reliving the whole thing, like in a movie or anything. I don't want to make it sound more dramatic than it really is.

Um, well, I guess... there is one other little thing. I really try not to let it affect my routine too much, I promise! But, um... I've started kind of taking the back hallway when I need to get around the building.

Um, well... my relationships are mostly fine, I think! I really try to be a good team player, and I definitely haven't had any big arguments with anyone or anything like that.

I just figure it's better to avoid getting worked up if I don't have to. I just hope they know I'm trying my best and that I'm not actually mad at them.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant difficulties in your daily life or work since the incident, beyond just being jumpy from loud noises?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Um, well... I really want to emphasize that I am still showing up for all my shifts! I haven't called out sick or anything like that. I know how important it is to be reliable, especially right now. 

But, um... I guess things have been just a little bit harder than usual. I've been a little slower at the reservation desk—just double-checking things more than I used to, to make sure I don't mess up. And, um, like I mentioned about the lobby... I might be finding a few little excuses to not go out there when guests need help, if I can avoid it.

I didn't ask for it, I promise. I'm still trying my hardest to do a good job. It's just taking me a little longer to get back to 100 percent, you know? That's okay, right? description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I’m usually pretty careful with coworkers, but lately I hear myself snapping short answers over little things, like someone asking me to repeat a confirmation number.\n- key: S082\n  name: Depersonalization_Derealization\n  description: my manager had to shorten a couple of my shifts because I’m slower at the reservation desk and I keep finding excuses not to cross the lobby where guests need help.\n- key: duration\n  name: receptionist_or_information_clerk / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S080\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident with the guest?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I guess... there is one other little thing. So the back hallway is just easier right now. \n\nI know it's probably silly to avoid it, and I'm sure I'll get over it soon. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm doing that for now? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these reactions to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, let's see... I think it's been about two weeks? Yeah, right around two weeks. It started pretty much right after... well, after that incident with the guest who got really upset and threatened me at the front desk. \n\nI really thought I'd be completely over it by now, honestly. I feel kind of bad that it's still bothering me even a little bit after a couple of weeks. I mean, nobody got hurt or anything, so I should be fine, right? I'm really trying my best to just move on from it. Is two weeks a normal amount of time to still be a little jumpy about something like that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things you've stopped doing because of the way you react to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I haven't stopped doing anything completely! I'm definitely still showing up for work every day. I really don't want to let my team down, you know? \n\nBut... I guess there have been a few little changes. I hope this doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, but... my manager actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts recently. It's just that I've been a little slower at the reservation desk than usual, just trying to keep my focus. And, um, like I mentioned about avoiding that spot... I kind of keep finding excuses not to cross the main lobby when guests need help out there. I'll ask a coworker to run out instead, or I'll just find some paperwork to organize behind the desk. \n\nI feel really terrible about it, honestly. I want to be helpful and do my job right. Do you think it's okay that my manager cut my hours a little bit until I get back to normal? I just want to make sure I'm handling all of this the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have vivid mental images of the incident with the guest at the front desk that feel like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm completely reliving the whole thing, like in a movie or anything. I don't want to make it sound more dramatic than it really is. \n\nBut, um... sometimes, yeah. Out of nowhere, I'll just get this really clear picture in my head of... of him leaning over the counter and knocking all the equipment toward me. It's weird, because it happens even when I'm just trying to answer a completely normal phone call from a nice guest. I'll just suddenly see it again for a second. \n\nI try to just blink it away and keep smiling, you know? I don't want anyone to notice I'm distracted. I wouldn't say I avoid *everywhere*, exactly. I try really hard not to let it affect my work too much, you know? I want to be a good employee. But... I guess I have been changing my routine just a little bit. \n\nLike, instead of walking through the main lobby where... where it happened, I've been taking the back hallway to get to the breakroom or the restroom. It's just... walking past that specific spot, it makes my chest feel a little tight, and I just get this strong urge to get away from it. So the back hallway is just easier right now. \n\nI know it's probably silly to avoid it, and I'm sure I'll get over it soon. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm doing that for now? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these reactions to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, let's see... I think it's been about two weeks? Yeah, right around two weeks. It started pretty much right after... well, after that incident with the guest who got really upset and threatened me at the front desk. \n\nI really thought I'd be completely over it by now, honestly. I feel kind of bad that it's still bothering me even a little bit after a couple of weeks. I mean, nobody got hurt or anything, so I should be fine, right? I'm really trying my best to just move on from it. Is two weeks a normal amount of time to still be a little jumpy about something like that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things you've stopped doing because of the way you react to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I haven't stopped doing anything completely! I'm definitely still showing up for work every day. I really don't want to let my team down, you know? \n\nBut... I guess there have been a few little changes. I hope this doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, but... my manager actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts recently. It's just that I've been a little slower at the reservation desk than usual, just trying to keep my focus. And, um, like I mentioned about avoiding that spot... I kind of keep finding excuses not to cross the main lobby when guests need help out there. I'll ask a coworker to run out instead, or I'll just find some paperwork to organize behind the desk. \n\nI feel really terrible about it, honestly. I want to be helpful and do my job right. Do you think it's okay that my manager cut my hours a little bit until I get back to normal? I just want to make sure I'm handling all of this the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have vivid mental images of the incident with the guest at the front desk that feel like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm completely reliving the whole thing, like in a movie or anything. I don't want to make it sound more dramatic than it really is. \n\nBut, um... sometimes, yeah. Out of nowhere, I'll just get this really clear picture in my head of... of him leaning over the counter and knocking all the equipment toward me. It's weird, because it happens even when I'm just trying to answer a completely normal phone call from a nice guest. I'll just suddenly see it again for a second. \n\nI try to just blink it away and keep smiling, you know? I don't want anyone to notice I'm distracted. I'm sure it's just my brain working through it, right? It's normal to have a few memories pop up like that after something stressful happens, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident with the guest?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I guess... there is one other little thing. I really try not to let it affect my routine too much, I promise! But, um... I've started kind of taking the back hallway when I need to get around the building. \n\nIt's just... whenever I have to walk past that exact spot in the main lobby where he stood, my chest gets all tight, and I just get this really strong urge to walk away from it as fast as I can. So the back hallway is just... easier right now. It doesn't take that much longer to go that way, so it's not a big deal, right? I just figure it's better to avoid getting worked up if I don't have to. That makes sense, doesn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since the incident?\n\nPatient: Um, well... my relationships are mostly fine, I think! I really try to be a good team player, and I definitely haven't had any big arguments with anyone or anything like that. \n\nBut, um... I guess I have been feeling just a tiny bit on edge at work. I don't want to sound like a bad coworker, because I'm usually pretty careful with everyone... but lately, I kind of hear myself snapping short answers over really little things. Like, um, if a coworker just asks me to repeat a guest's confirmation number, I'll catch myself being a little short with them. \n\nI always feel awful right after! I just hope they know I'm trying my best and that I'm not actually mad at them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your daily life or work since the incident, beyond just being jumpy from loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really want to emphasize that I am still showing up for all my shifts! I haven't called out sick or anything like that. I know how important it is to be reliable, especially right now. \n\nBut, um... I guess things have been just a little bit harder than usual. I've been a little slower at the reservation desk—just double-checking things more than I used to, to make sure I don't mess up. And, um, like I mentioned about the lobby... I might be finding a few little excuses to not go out there when guests need help, if I can avoid it. \n\nMy manager actually noticed, and she... well, she shortened a couple of my shifts this week. She said it was just to give me a little breather, which was really nice of her! I didn't ask for it, I promise. I'm still trying my hardest to do a good job. It's just taking me a little longer to get back to 100 percent, you know? That's okay, right? I'm really trying to keep everything as normal as possible!\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest because it makes you feel upset or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Um, well... no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding it! I definitely don't mind answering questions about it if people need to know. \n\nI guess... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, I'm definitely still showing up to work! I haven't missed a single day, I promise. I mean, my manager did... well, she shortened a couple of my shifts recently. But that's just because I've been a little slower at the reservation desk, you know? Just trying to be extra careful and make sure I get everything exactly right. 

And, um, I guess I have been finding a few little excuses to not walk across the main lobby when guests need help over there. But that's just because I'm trying to stay focused on my tasks behind the desk, right? It's important to keep the desk covered. So I'm still functioning fine, really! Just making a few small adjustments. That's a totally normal way to handle things for now, isn't it?

And then it takes me a minute or so to just get my heart rate back down to normal. \n\nI try to hide it so people don't think I'm acting strange, but it's been bothering me. Is that... is that something you see people for? I just want to make sure I'm not overreacting by being here.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge in situations that don't involve loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't think so, anyway. Honestly, I'm usually a pretty relaxed guy. It really just seems to be the sudden noises that get to me. \n\nIf it's just regular office chatter or quiet, I feel completely fine. I hope that makes sense? I just... I don't want to make it sound like I'm a nervous wreck about everything, because I'm really not. It's just those loud, sudden things. Am I explaining that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving a past event when you hear those loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I guess, maybe a little bit? I really hope I don't sound crazy saying this, but... yeah. \n\nSometimes, just out of nowhere, I’ll get this picture in my head of... well, of that guest leaning over the counter and knocking all the equipment toward me. And it's weird because it'll happen even when I’m just trying to answer a normal, everyday call. It’s usually just a quick flash, you know? Nothing too dramatic, so I try to just smile and push past it. \n\nIs that... does that answer your question okay? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because of the possibility of hearing loud noises?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say I avoid *everywhere*, exactly. I try really hard not to let it affect my work too much, you know? I want to be a good employee. But... I guess I have been changing my routine just a little bit. \n\nLike, instead of walking through the main lobby where... where it happened, I've been taking the back hallway to get to the breakroom or the restroom. It's just... walking past that specific spot, it makes my chest feel a little tight, and I just get this strong urge to get away from it. So the back hallway is just easier right now. \n\nI know it's probably silly to avoid it, and I'm sure I'll get over it soon. I just... I hope it's okay that I'm doing that for now? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these reactions to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, let's see... I think it's been about two weeks? Yeah, right around two weeks. It started pretty much right after... well, after that incident with the guest who got really upset and threatened me at the front desk. \n\nI really thought I'd be completely over it by now, honestly. I feel kind of bad that it's still bothering me even a little bit after a couple of weeks. I mean, nobody got hurt or anything, so I should be fine, right? I'm really trying my best to just move on from it. Is two weeks a normal amount of time to still be a little jumpy about something like that?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or things you've stopped doing because of the way you react to loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I haven't stopped doing anything completely! I'm definitely still showing up for work every day. I really don't want to let my team down, you know? \n\nBut... I guess there have been a few little changes. I hope this doesn't make me sound like a bad employee, but... my manager actually had to shorten a couple of my shifts recently. It's just that I've been a little slower at the reservation desk than usual, just trying to keep my focus. And, um, like I mentioned about avoiding that spot... I kind of keep finding excuses not to cross the main lobby when guests need help out there. I'll ask a coworker to run out instead, or I'll just find some paperwork to organize behind the desk. \n\nI feel really terrible about it, honestly. I want to be helpful and do my job right. Do you think it's okay that my manager cut my hours a little bit until I get back to normal? I just want to make sure I'm handling all of this the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have vivid mental images of the incident with the guest at the front desk that feel like you're reliving it?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I wouldn't say I'm completely reliving the whole thing, like in a movie or anything. I don't want to make it sound more dramatic than it really is. \n\nBut, um... sometimes, yeah. Out of nowhere, I'll just get this really clear picture in my head of... of him leaning over the counter and knocking all the equipment toward me. It's weird, because it happens even when I'm just trying to answer a completely normal phone call from a nice guest. I'll just suddenly see it again for a second. \n\nI try to just blink it away and keep smiling, you know? I don't want anyone to notice I'm distracted. I'm sure it's just my brain working through it, right? It's normal to have a few memories pop up like that after something stressful happens, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities you've been avoiding because they remind you of the incident with the guest?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I guess... there is one other little thing. I really try not to let it affect my routine too much, I promise! But, um... I've started kind of taking the back hallway when I need to get around the building. \n\nIt's just... whenever I have to walk past that exact spot in the main lobby where he stood, my chest gets all tight, and I just get this really strong urge to walk away from it as fast as I can. So the back hallway is just... easier right now. It doesn't take that much longer to go that way, so it's not a big deal, right? I just figure it's better to avoid getting worked up if I don't have to. That makes sense, doesn't it?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or coworkers since the incident?\n\nPatient: Um, well... my relationships are mostly fine, I think! I really try to be a good team player, and I definitely haven't had any big arguments with anyone or anything like that. \n\nBut, um... I guess I have been feeling just a tiny bit on edge at work. I don't want to sound like a bad coworker, because I'm usually pretty careful with everyone... but lately, I kind of hear myself snapping short answers over really little things. Like, um, if a coworker just asks me to repeat a guest's confirmation number, I'll catch myself being a little short with them. \n\nI always feel awful right after! I really don't mean to be rude, I just... my patience is a little thinner right now, I guess. That's a normal reaction when you're stressed out, right? I just hope they know I'm trying my best and that I'm not actually mad at them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your daily life or work since the incident, beyond just being jumpy from loud noises?\n\nPatient: Um, well... I really want to emphasize that I am still showing up for all my shifts! I haven't called out sick or anything like that. I know how important it is to be reliable, especially right now. \n\nBut, um... I guess things have been just a little bit harder than usual. I've been a little slower at the reservation desk—just double-checking things more than I used to, to make sure I don't mess up. And, um, like I mentioned about the lobby... I might be finding a few little excuses to not go out there when guests need help, if I can avoid it. \n\nMy manager actually noticed, and she... well, she shortened a couple of my shifts this week. She said it was just to give me a little breather, which was really nice of her! I didn't ask for it, I promise. I'm still trying my hardest to do a good job. It's just taking me a little longer to get back to 100 percent, you know? That's okay, right? As long as I'm still trying?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares about what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Um, no, no nightmares at all! I've actually been sleeping okay, I think. I mean, maybe tossing and turning a little bit just from normal stress, but definitely no bad dreams about the incident or anything like that. \n\nI know some people get really awful nightmares after something stressful happens, so I'm really grateful that hasn't been an issue for me. I try to wind down with a book or some quiet music before bed so I don't go to sleep upset. That's a good habit to have anyway, right? So yeah, my sleep is pretty much fine, I promise!\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific things or activities that you used to enjoy but have stopped doing altogether since the incident with the guest?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really! I'm still doing all my usual things, I think. I mean, I really try to keep my routine going because I know that's supposed to be healthy, right? \n\nI still watch my favorite shows, and I still like cooking dinner when I get home. I haven't really stopped doing anything I enjoy. I mean, maybe I'm a little more tired after work right now, so I might just stay in a bit more, but that's just because I want to rest up so I can be ready for my next shift. That's perfectly normal, isn't it? Just needing a little extra downtime? \n\nBut as far as actually stopping things I like... no, definitely not. I'm really trying to keep everything as normal as possible!\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking or thinking about the incident with the guest because it makes you feel upset or uncomfortable?\n\nPatient: Um, well... no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding it! I mean, I'm talking to you about it right now, right? And I went over the whole thing with my manager right after it happened. I definitely don't mind answering questions about it if people need to know. \n\nI guess... I try not to just sit around and dwell on it when I'm off the clock, but that's really just because I want to keep a positive attitude. It's healthier to focus on moving forward rather than getting stuck on one bad day, isn't it? I think it's good to just keep looking ahead. So, yeah, I'm not running away from it or hiding from it, I promise. I'm completely willing to talk about it whenever we need to! That's the right way to handle it, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts that you're to blame for what happened with the guest at the front desk?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not! I mean, I know it wasn't my fault. I was just doing my job, following the hotel policies, right? \n\nI mean, maybe I could have... I don't know, maybe smiled a little more or tried to de-escalate things faster? Sometimes I wonder if I had just given him the upgrade he wanted right away, it wouldn't have gotten so out of hand. But that's just me trying to be better at my job, you know? Always looking for ways to improve customer service. \n\nBut as far as actually blaming myself... no, I know I didn't cause him to act that way. People just get stressed when they travel, and I just happened to be the one at the desk. I'm really trying to just see it as a learning experience, which is the best way to look at it, right? So, yeah, I'm definitely not beating myself up over it or anything like that!\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your reactions to loud noises or memories of the incident have significantly impacted your ability to function at work or in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, well, I'm definitely still showing up to work! I haven't missed a single day, I promise. I mean, my manager did... well, she shortened a couple of my shifts recently. But that's just because I've been a little slower at the reservation desk, you know? Just trying to be extra careful and make sure I get everything exactly right. \n\nAnd, um, I guess I have been finding a few little excuses to not walk across the main lobby when guests need help over there. But that's just because I'm trying to stay focused on my tasks behind the desk, right? It's important to keep the desk covered. So I'm still functioning fine, really! Just making a few small adjustments. That's a totally normal way to handle things for now, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other places, activities, or situations that you're avoiding because they remind you of what happened with the guest?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really avoiding anything! I mean, I'm still coming to work and everything, like I said. \n\nI guess... well, I have been taking the back hallway to get to the breakroom lately, instead of walking through the main lobby. But that's just because it's a little faster, you know? And it keeps me out of the guests' way when I'm on my break. \n\nI mean, if I'm being completely honest, walking right past that spot where he started yelling... it does make my chest feel a little tight, and I just kind of want to hurry past it. But it's totally fine! I just take the back way so I don't have to deal with that feeling, and then I can just relax on my break. That's a pretty smart way to handle it, don't you think? Just keeping things peaceful?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any persistent negative thoughts about yourself or others since the incident?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not. I mean, I try to be a really positive person, you know? I always try to see the good in people, especially working in hospitality. \n\nI guess... well, sometimes when I see a guest looking frustrated at the desk, I might catch myself thinking, \"Oh no, here we go again, people are just so unreasonable.\" But I catch myself right away! I know that's not true, and most of our guests are wonderful. It's just a fleeting thought, really. \n\nAnd as for myself, I'm just focusing on being the best employee I can be. I mean, I'm not walking around thinking I'm terrible at my job or anything. I'm just trying to learn from what happened. That's a good attitude to have, right? Just staying positive and focusing on the good things?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
